And I don’t think I’ve had the sense that my thoughts belong to someone else or anything like that.\n\nOutside of the eating, I don’t really recognize that experience. If anything, it’s more like ordinary distraction or being preoccupied, not a scary detached feeling. So… no, I haven’t noticed that happening when I’m not eating.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was unreal or distant, even when you weren’t in the middle of an eating episode?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I have—not really. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, like whether things around me have ever seemed unreal or far away even when I’m not eating, and I can’t point to an experience like that.\n\nI do have times where I’m tired or my mind is somewhere else and I’m not fully taking in the room or what’s happening, but that feels more like distraction than the world feeling unreal. I don’t remember looking around and thinking everything seemed strange or distant in that way.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no. If anything like that has happened, it hasn’t been clear enough or upsetting enough for me to recognize it as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a dream or that the world around you wasn’t real, even when you weren’t eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I know you’ve asked it a couple different ways, and I’m trying not to just automatically say no without thinking, but… when I hear “like I’m in a dream” or “the world isn’t real,” that doesn’t really match anything I can remember outside of eating.\n\nLike, I’ve definitely had normal moments where I’m tired and sort of spaced out, or I’m going through the motions and not paying close attention, but it still feels like real life. I don’t look around and feel like the room is fake or like I’m dreaming while awake. It’s more just me being distracted or worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nSo my honest answer is that I haven’t noticed that happening when I’m not eating. If it has happened in some very mild way, I don’t think I registered it as strange or frightening or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you felt unreal during times of stress, sadness, or emotional upset, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing. Stress or sadness definitely makes me feel overwhelmed sometimes, and I can get kind of inward-focused, like I’m stuck in my own head and not really paying attention to everything around me. But I wouldn’t call it feeling detached from myself, exactly.\n\nLike, if I’m upset, I might sit there and feel sort of numb or shut down for a bit, or I’m quiet and not really engaging, but I still know where I am and I still feel like me. The room doesn’t feel unreal, and I don’t feel like I’m outside my body watching myself or anything like that.\n\nI’m trying to think of whether there’s been a time when I was really emotional and everything felt strange or dreamlike, and I just… I can’t really come up with one. It’s more like being sad, tired, preoccupied, or overwhelmed. So I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed that happening, even during emotional stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you felt unreal during times of stress, sadness, or emotional upset, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah… I think my answer is still no, at least not in any clear way. I might not be paying attention to things around me because I’m upset, but the room still feels real, people still feel real, everything still feels like it’s actually happening. Sometimes I’ll be so focused on what I’m feeling that I’m not really taking in everything around me, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed the kitchen looking strange or the room feeling fake or distant in that way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m not really fully present because I’m caught up in the episode, if that makes sense. I don’t remember feeling like the world around me wasn’t real.\n\nSo… I guess the best answer is I haven’t really noticed that. Detached from myself, maybe in the sense of being kind of on autopilot, but not so much the surroundings feeling unreal.\n\nDoctor: When you’re having one of these episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the kitchen, the food, or the room — feel strange, fake, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked this a couple different ways, and I’m trying not to over-answer it, but… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of the kitchen or the food or the room seeming fake or unreal.\n\nDuring an episode, it’s more like I’m really locked into what I’m doing. My attention gets very narrow, almost like everything else is just kind of pushed to the side. So I might not be noticing the room very much, or I might not be thinking about anything except getting more food or continuing, but if I actually think about it, the things around me still feel real. The kitchen is still the kitchen. The food doesn’t look strange to me, and the room doesn’t feel like a movie set or a dream or anything like that.\n\nI guess the detached feeling, if I use that word, is more about me not feeling fully in control or fully present with myself, rather than the outside world changing. It’s not like I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” or “Something is off with the room.” It’s more that I’m caught up in the eating and sort of tuned out from everything else. So no, I haven’t really noticed the surroundings feeling strange or fake.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the middle of one of these eating episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the food, the kitchen, or the room — feel strange, distant, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still landing on… no, not really. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think about that outside of the eating episodes, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there’s some small moment I’m forgetting. But… I don’t think I’ve had that feeling, no. Not like I’m not real, or the world isn’t real.

I do get very overwhelmed sometimes, or upset, and I can feel kind of shut down or like I need to sit quietly and not deal with anything for a bit. But I still know where I am, and things around me still feel normal. It’s more like I’m emotionally overloaded, not like reality changes or I’m watching myself from somewhere else.

There have probably been times when I’ve felt foggy or spaced out when I’m stressed, but I wouldn’t describe it as the world seeming fake, or me feeling unreal. I’ve never really had a moment where I looked around and thought, “This isn’t real,” or felt like I disappeared from myself. So as far as I can tell, no, I haven’t noticed that happening outside of eating either.

The room doesn’t feel unreal, and I don’t feel like I’m outside my body watching myself or anything like that.\n\nI’m trying to think of whether there’s been a time when I was really emotional and everything felt strange or dreamlike, and I just… I can’t really come up with one. It’s more like being sad, tired, preoccupied, or overwhelmed. So I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed that happening, even during emotional stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you felt unreal during times of stress, sadness, or emotional upset, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah… I think my answer is still no, at least not in any clear way. When I’m stressed or sad, I can get very caught up in my thoughts, and I might be quiet or sort of withdrawn, but I don’t remember feeling like I wasn’t myself or like I was watching myself from the outside.\n\nAnd with the world feeling unreal—no, I don’t think so. I might not be paying attention to things around me because I’m upset, but the room still feels real, people still feel real, everything still feels like it’s actually happening. It’s more like I’m overwhelmed or distracted, not like I’m detached from reality.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I’m just trying to be accurate. I can understand the difference you’re asking about, and I don’t think that’s something I’ve experienced, even during emotional upset.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not real or that the world around you was not real during times of emotional distress, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. And I know I’ve basically answered a version of this already, so I’m trying to be careful and not just repeat myself automatically. But when you say “not real” or “the world wasn’t real,” that sounds like something pretty specific, and I don’t think that’s what happens to me.\n\nWhen I’m emotionally upset, I can definitely get quiet, or sort of shut down, or feel like I’m just sitting with too much in my head. Sometimes I’ll be so focused on what I’m feeling that I’m not really taking in everything around me, if that makes sense. Like someone could be talking and I hear them, but I’m not really fully engaged because I’m upset or distracted. But it still feels like me sitting there, in a real room, with real people. I don’t feel unreal to myself.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had that dreamlike feeling or like I’m outside of things watching it happen. It’s more just… emotional overwhelm, I guess, or being preoccupied. So my answer would be no, not that I’ve noticed during emotional distress when I’m not eating.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the middle of one of these eating episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the world around you—like the kitchen, the food, the room—feels unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say the room or the kitchen feels unreal, exactly. In the middle of it, I can feel very… narrowed in, I guess. Like my attention is just on the food and what I’m doing, and everything else kind of fades into the background. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed the kitchen looking strange or the room feeling fake or distant in that way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m not really fully present because I’m caught up in the episode, if that makes sense. I might not be thinking clearly about the space around me, or I’m not paying attention to the details, but if I stop and look around, it still feels like my kitchen, my counter, the food is real. I don’t remember feeling like the world around me wasn’t real.\n\nSo… I guess the best answer is I haven’t really noticed that. Detached from myself, maybe in the sense of being kind of on autopilot, but not so much the surroundings feeling unreal.\n\nDoctor: When you’re having one of these episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the kitchen, the food, or the room — feel strange, fake, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked this a couple different ways, and I’m trying not to over-answer it, but… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of the kitchen or the food or the room seeming fake or unreal.\n\nDuring an episode, it’s more like I’m really locked into what I’m doing. My attention gets very narrow, almost like everything else is just kind of pushed to the side. So I might not be noticing the room very much, or I might not be thinking about anything except getting more food or continuing, but if I actually think about it, the things around me still feel real. The kitchen is still the kitchen. The food doesn’t look strange to me, and the room doesn’t feel like a movie set or a dream or anything like that.\n\nI guess the detached feeling, if I use that word, is more about me not feeling fully in control or fully present with myself, rather than the outside world changing. It’s not like I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” or “Something is off with the room.” It’s more that I’m caught up in the eating and sort of tuned out from everything else. So no, I haven’t really noticed the surroundings feeling strange or fake.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the middle of one of these eating episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the food, the kitchen, or the room — feel strange, distant, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still landing on… no, not really. It’s more that I’m so focused on eating that I’m not really paying attention to anything else. The room is there, the counters are there, the food is definitely real to me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel kind of overwhelmed or like I’m not fully “with it,” if that makes sense, but not like I’m literally outside of myself watching from across the room or from above or something like that.

I’m trying to be careful, because sometimes people say “outside yourself” in a more everyday way, like “I didn’t feel like myself,” and that I can relate to a little more. Like if I’m stressed or upset, I might feel quiet, checked out, or like I’m just trying to get through the next few minutes. But I still feel like I’m me, in my body, doing whatever I’m doing. I don’t have that sense of observing myself from a distance, like it’s happening to someone else.

So if you mean that more specific feeling of being completely separate from myself and watching my own actions, then no, I haven’t noticed that when I’m not eating.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’m scared of what happens when I start eating sometimes. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but especially when I’m alone in the kitchen, it’s like something shifts. I’ll start eating and then I can feel myself sort of… not in control of it anymore.\n\nAnd the whole time I’m thinking, Janet, stop. Put it away. Close the cabinet, walk away. But I don’t. It feels like I’ve stepped aside from myself, almost, and I’m watching myself keep going even though I really don’t want to. That’s the part that scares me the most — not just the eating, but that feeling that I can’t make myself stop once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens when you feel like you can't stop eating? Do you feel like you're losing control, or is it more that you don't want to stop even though part of you knows you should?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more like losing control. I mean, there may be a tiny part of me that wants to keep eating in the moment, but that’s not the main feeling. The main feeling is panic, almost, because once I’ve started—especially if I’m by myself in the kitchen—it’s like I can’t get myself to stop.\n\nI’ll be standing there telling myself, Janet, put it away. Close the bag. Put it back in the cabinet. Just walk out of the room. And I can hear myself thinking it, very clearly, but my hands keep going anyway. That’s what makes it feel so frightening to me. It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Oh, I don’t care, I’ll just keep eating.” It feels more like I’ve stepped aside from myself and I’m watching it happen, and I can’t quite get back in control of my own body.\n\nDoctor: Before these episodes start, do you notice any specific feelings or situations that seem to bring them on—like stress, being alone, or certain emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I’ve tried to look for a pattern because I keep thinking if I could just figure out what sets it off, maybe I could head it off before it starts. But it doesn’t always feel obvious to me.\n\nBeing alone might be part of it, but I don’t know if that’s the trigger or just when it’s easier for it to happen because no one’s watching. Sometimes I’ll be in the kitchen and it feels almost ordinary at first, like I’m just getting something to eat, and then somewhere along the way it changes. But I can’t always point to one feeling beforehand and say, “That’s it, that’s what caused it.”\n\nI mean, I suppose I’m not usually feeling great about myself when it happens, but that’s kind of vague, isn’t it? I don’t know if it’s stress or sadness or boredom or what. I wish I could give you a cleaner answer. It’s more like I notice it once I’m already in it, rather than clearly seeing what came right before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced that feeling of being disconnected from yourself or watching yourself from the outside in situations other than when you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in the same way. I’m trying to think carefully, because I don’t want to say no too quickly and then realize I’ve missed something.\n\nI mean, I’ve had moments where I’m distracted or sort of on autopilot, like anyone does, where you drive somewhere and don’t remember every turn, or you’re doing dishes and your mind is somewhere else. But that doesn’t feel like what happens with the eating. That feels more ordinary, I guess.\n\nThe kitchen thing feels much more upsetting because I’m aware of it while it’s happening. I’m not blanking out. I’m actually very aware, and I’m telling myself to stop, and it’s like there’s this distance between the part of me thinking and the part of me doing. I don’t really notice that in other situations, or if I do, it’s not something that has stood out to me enough that I’d call it the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve experienced it outside of eating, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or as if you were observing yourself from outside during times when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in any clear way. I mean, I know I’ve already kind of described that “watching myself” feeling with eating, but outside of that, I can’t really think of times where I’ve felt like I was outside my body or observing myself the way you’re asking.\n\nThere are normal times where I’m distracted or not fully present, like if I’m tired or my mind is wandering, but that feels different to me. It doesn’t have that same upsetting quality, and I’m not standing there thinking, “Why can’t I get back in control of myself?” like I do when I’m eating.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve noticed. If it’s happened, it hasn’t stood out to me as something separate or unusual.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your surroundings, as if things around you were unreal, or that your body or thoughts didn’t belong to you, even when you weren’t eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not the way you’re describing it. I’m trying to really check myself here, because when you say “unreal” or “thoughts didn’t belong to you,” that sounds pretty specific, and I don’t want to just brush past it.\n\nI’ve had times where I’m tired or overwhelmed and things feel sort of dull or foggy, like I’m not paying close attention to what’s around me, but I wouldn’t say the room feels unreal or like my body isn’t mine. And I don’t think I’ve had the sense that my thoughts belong to someone else or anything like that.\n\nOutside of the eating, I don’t really recognize that experience. If anything, it’s more like ordinary distraction or being preoccupied, not a scary detached feeling. So… no, I haven’t noticed that happening when I’m not eating.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like the world around you was unreal or distant, even when you weren’t in the middle of an eating episode?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I have—not really. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, like whether things around me have ever seemed unreal or far away even when I’m not eating, and I can’t point to an experience like that.\n\nI do have times where I’m tired or my mind is somewhere else and I’m not fully taking in the room or what’s happening, but that feels more like distraction than the world feeling unreal. I don’t remember looking around and thinking everything seemed strange or distant in that way.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no. If anything like that has happened, it hasn’t been clear enough or upsetting enough for me to recognize it as that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a dream or that the world around you wasn’t real, even when you weren’t eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I know you’ve asked it a couple different ways, and I’m trying not to just automatically say no without thinking, but… when I hear “like I’m in a dream” or “the world isn’t real,” that doesn’t really match anything I can remember outside of eating.\n\nLike, I’ve definitely had normal moments where I’m tired and sort of spaced out, or I’m going through the motions and not paying close attention, but it still feels like real life. I don’t look around and feel like the room is fake or like I’m dreaming while awake. It’s more just me being distracted or worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nSo my honest answer is that I haven’t noticed that happening when I’m not eating. If it has happened in some very mild way, I don’t think I registered it as strange or frightening or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you felt unreal during times of stress, sadness, or emotional upset, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing. Stress or sadness definitely makes me feel overwhelmed sometimes, and I can get kind of inward-focused, like I’m stuck in my own head and not really paying attention to everything around me. But I wouldn’t call it feeling detached from myself, exactly.\n\nLike, if I’m upset, I might sit there and feel sort of numb or shut down for a bit, or I’m quiet and not really engaging, but I still know where I am and I still feel like me. The room doesn’t feel unreal, and I don’t feel like I’m outside my body watching myself or anything like that.\n\nI’m trying to think of whether there’s been a time when I was really emotional and everything felt strange or dreamlike, and I just… I can’t really come up with one. It’s more like being sad, tired, preoccupied, or overwhelmed. So I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed that happening, even during emotional stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you felt unreal during times of stress, sadness, or emotional upset, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah… I think my answer is still no, at least not in any clear way. When I’m stressed or sad, I can get very caught up in my thoughts, and I might be quiet or sort of withdrawn, but I don’t remember feeling like I wasn’t myself or like I was watching myself from the outside.\n\nAnd with the world feeling unreal—no, I don’t think so. I might not be paying attention to things around me because I’m upset, but the room still feels real, people still feel real, everything still feels like it’s actually happening. It’s more like I’m overwhelmed or distracted, not like I’m detached from reality.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I’m just trying to be accurate. I can understand the difference you’re asking about, and I don’t think that’s something I’ve experienced, even during emotional upset.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not real or that the world around you was not real during times of emotional distress, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. And I know I’ve basically answered a version of this already, so I’m trying to be careful and not just repeat myself automatically. But when you say “not real” or “the world wasn’t real,” that sounds like something pretty specific, and I don’t think that’s what happens to me.\n\nWhen I’m emotionally upset, I can definitely get quiet, or sort of shut down, or feel like I’m just sitting with too much in my head. Sometimes I’ll be so focused on what I’m feeling that I’m not really taking in everything around me, if that makes sense. Like someone could be talking and I hear them, but I’m not really fully engaged because I’m upset or distracted. But it still feels like me sitting there, in a real room, with real people. I don’t feel unreal to myself.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had that dreamlike feeling or like I’m outside of things watching it happen. It’s more just… emotional overwhelm, I guess, or being preoccupied. So my answer would be no, not that I’ve noticed during emotional distress when I’m not eating.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the middle of one of these eating episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the world around you—like the kitchen, the food, the room—feels unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say the room or the kitchen feels unreal, exactly. In the middle of it, I can feel very… narrowed in, I guess. Like my attention is just on the food and what I’m doing, and everything else kind of fades into the background. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed the kitchen looking strange or the room feeling fake or distant in that way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m not really fully present because I’m caught up in the episode, if that makes sense. I might not be thinking clearly about the space around me, or I’m not paying attention to the details, but if I stop and look around, it still feels like my kitchen, my counter, the food is real. I don’t remember feeling like the world around me wasn’t real.\n\nSo… I guess the best answer is I haven’t really noticed that. Detached from myself, maybe in the sense of being kind of on autopilot, but not so much the surroundings feeling unreal.\n\nDoctor: When you’re having one of these episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the kitchen, the food, or the room — feel strange, fake, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked this a couple different ways, and I’m trying not to over-answer it, but… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of the kitchen or the food or the room seeming fake or unreal.\n\nDuring an episode, it’s more like I’m really locked into what I’m doing. The kitchen is still the kitchen. It’s not like I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” or “Something is off with the room.” It’s more that I’m caught up in the eating and sort of tuned out from everything else. So no, I haven’t really noticed the surroundings feeling strange or fake.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the middle of one of these eating episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the food, the kitchen, or the room — feel strange, distant, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still landing on… no, not really. No, I don’t think I’ve had that. I know we’ve kind of been circling around the same idea, and I’m trying to answer it the way you mean it, not just in a casual “I felt out of it” way.

There are definitely times where I can feel tired, overwhelmed, or mentally kind of checked out, but I still feel like I’m in my body. I don’t have that sense of being over there watching myself, or like my body is doing things and I’m somewhere else observing it. Even briefly, I can’t really think of a moment like that outside of eating.

So I’d say no — not completely detached from my body or watching myself from outside. At least not in any way I’ve noticed or would describe that way.

It’s more like being sad, tired, preoccupied, or overwhelmed. I might not be paying attention to things around me because I’m upset, but the room still feels real, people still feel real, everything still feels like it’s actually happening. It’s more like I’m overwhelmed or distracted, not like I’m detached from reality.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, I’m just trying to be accurate. I can understand the difference you’re asking about, and I don’t think that’s something I’ve experienced, even during emotional upset.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not real or that the world around you was not real during times of emotional distress, even when you weren't eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. In the middle of it, I can feel very… narrowed in, I guess. Like my attention is just on the food and what I’m doing, and everything else kind of fades into the background. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed the kitchen looking strange or the room feeling fake or distant in that way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m not really fully present because I’m caught up in the episode, if that makes sense. I might not be thinking clearly about the space around me, or I’m not paying attention to the details, but if I stop and look around, it still feels like my kitchen, my counter, the food is real. I don’t remember feeling like the world around me wasn’t real.\n\nSo… I guess the best answer is I haven’t really noticed that. Detached from myself, maybe in the sense of being kind of on autopilot, but not so much the surroundings feeling unreal.\n\nDoctor: When you’re having one of these episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the kitchen, the food, or the room — feel strange, fake, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked this a couple different ways, and I’m trying not to over-answer it, but… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of the kitchen or the food or the room seeming fake or unreal.\n\nDuring an episode, it’s more like I’m really locked into what I’m doing. My attention gets very narrow, almost like everything else is just kind of pushed to the side. So I might not be noticing the room very much, or I might not be thinking about anything except getting more food or continuing, but if I actually think about it, the things around me still feel real. The kitchen is still the kitchen. The food doesn’t look strange to me, and the room doesn’t feel like a movie set or a dream or anything like that.\n\nI guess the detached feeling, if I use that word, is more about me not feeling fully in control or fully present with myself, rather than the outside world changing. It’s not like I look around and think, “This isn’t real,” or “Something is off with the room.” It’s more that I’m caught up in the eating and sort of tuned out from everything else. So no, I haven’t really noticed the surroundings feeling strange or fake.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the middle of one of these eating episodes and you feel detached from yourself, do you also feel like the things around you — like the food, the kitchen, or the room — feel strange, distant, or not quite real?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still landing on… no, not really. I know this is very similar to what you just asked, so I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, but I’m trying to answer the exact thing you mean.\n\nWhen I’m in the middle of it, the food and the kitchen don’t feel fake to me, or like they’re far away, or like I’m in a dream. It’s more that I’m so focused on eating that I’m not really paying attention to anything else. The room is there, the counters are there, the food is definitely real to me. It doesn’t have that strange “not quite real” feeling.\n\nIf anything feels off, it’s more inside me — like I’m not pausing and thinking normally, or I’m just going through the motions. But the things around me don’t seem unreal or distorted. I don’t look at the kitchen and think, “This feels wrong,” or “This isn’t really happening.” It’s just that I’m very caught up in what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced moments where you felt detached from yourself or that the world felt unreal during or after these eating episodes, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I’ve experienced it in the way you’re describing. During the episode, I can feel kind of disconnected from what I’m choosing, if that makes sense — like I’m not stopping myself or I’m not fully checking in with myself — but I don’t remember having a moment where I thought, “I’m not real,” or “the world isn’t real.”\n\nAfterward, I might feel sort of foggy or upset or like I wasn’t acting like myself, but even then the room and everything around me still feels normal. I’m aware of where I am. It doesn’t feel like a dream or like I’m outside of my body watching it happen. It’s more just… I get caught up in the eating, and then afterward I’m trying to process what happened.\n\nSo if you mean a true unreal feeling, even briefly, I don’t think so. At least not that I’ve noticed or could clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not real or that the world wasn’t real at any time when you weren’t eating, even for a brief moment, such as during or after feeling very anxious, upset, or stressed?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that outside of the eating episodes, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there’s some small moment I’m forgetting. But… I don’t think I’ve had that feeling, no. Not like I’m not real, or the world isn’t real.\n\nI do get very overwhelmed sometimes, or upset, and I can feel kind of shut down or like I need to sit quietly and not deal with anything for a bit. But I still know where I am, and things around me still feel normal. It’s more like I’m emotionally overloaded, not like reality changes or I’m watching myself from somewhere else.\n\nThere have probably been times when I’ve felt foggy or spaced out when I’m stressed, but I wouldn’t describe it as the world seeming fake, or me feeling unreal. I’ve never really had a moment where I looked around and thought, “This isn’t real,” or felt like I disappeared from myself. So as far as I can tell, no, I haven’t noticed that happening outside of eating either.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely outside of yourself, like you were watching your own actions from a distance, even when you weren’t eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel kind of overwhelmed or like I’m not fully “with it,” if that makes sense, but not like I’m literally outside of myself watching from across the room or from above or something like that.\n\nI’m trying to be careful, because sometimes people say “outside yourself” in a more everyday way, like “I didn’t feel like myself,” and that I can relate to a little more. Like if I’m stressed or upset, I might feel quiet, checked out, or like I’m just trying to get through the next few minutes. But I still feel like I’m me, in my body, doing whatever I’m doing. I don’t have that sense of observing myself from a distance, like it’s happening to someone else.\n\nSo if you mean that more specific feeling of being completely separate from myself and watching my own actions, then no, I haven’t noticed that when I’m not eating.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely detached from your body or that you were watching yourself from outside, even for a brief moment, during situations other than when you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. I know we’ve kind of been circling around the same idea, and I’m trying to answer it the way you mean it, not just in a casual “I felt out of it” way.\n\nThere are definitely times where I can feel tired, overwhelmed, or mentally kind of checked out, but I still feel like I’m in my body. I don’t have that sense of being over there watching myself, or like my body is doing things and I’m somewhere else observing it. Even briefly, I can’t really think of a moment like that outside of eating.\n\nSo I’d say no — not completely detached from my body or watching myself from outside. At least not in any way I’ve noticed or would describe that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think so, no. I know you’re asking specifically about anxiety or stress, and not when I’m eating, so I’m trying to separate that out in my mind.
